542Or, may he cut a collier’s throat with his razor by chance-medley, and yet be hanged for’t.

MOROSE

543I will forgive him, rather than hear any more. I beseech you, sir.

ACT 3 SCENE 6

[Enter] DAW, HAUGHTY, CENTAURE, MAVIS, TRUSTY.

DAW

544This way, madam.

MOROSE

545Oh, the sea breaks in upon me! Another flood! An inundation! I shall be o’erwhelmed with noise. It beats already at my shores. I feel an earthquake in myself for’t.

DAW

546[Kissing Epicene] ’Give you joy, mistress.

MOROSE

547Has she servants too?

DAW

548I have brought some ladies here to see and know you. She kisses them severally as he presents them. My lady Haughty, this my lady Centaure, Mistress Dol Mavis, Mistress Trusty, my lady Haughty’s woman. Where’s your husband? Let’s see him: can he endure no noise? Let me come to him.

MOROSE

549What nomenclator is this?

TRUEWIT

550Sir John Daw, sir, your wife’s servant, this.

MOROSE

551A Daw, and her servant! O, ’tis decreed, ’tis decreed of me, and she have such servants.

[He tries to leave]

TRUEWIT

552Nay sir, you must kiss the ladies, you must not go away now; they come toward you to seek you out.

HAUGHTY

553I’faith, Master Morose, would you steal a marriage thus, in the midst of so many friends, and not acquaint us? Well, I’ll kiss you, notwithstanding the justice of my quarrel. You shall give me leave, mistress, to use a becoming familiarity with your husband.

EPICENE

554Your ladyship does me an honour in it, to let me know he is so worthy your favour; as you have done both him and me grace, to visit so unprepared a pair to entertain you.

MOROSE

555Compliment! Compliment!

EPICENE

556But I must lay the burden of that upon my servant here.

HAUGHTY

557It shall not need, Mistress Morose; we will all bear, rather than one shall be oppressed.

MOROSE

558I know it, and you will teach her the faculty, if she be to learn it.

[The Collegiates talk apart with Truewit.]

HAUGHTY

559Is this the silent woman?

CENTAURE

560Nay, she has found her tongue since she was married, Master Truewit says.

HAUGHTY

561Oh, Master Truewit! ’Save you. What kind of creature is your bride here? She speaks, methinks!

TRUEWIT

562Yes, madam, believe it, she is a gentlewoman of very absolute behavior and of a good race.

HAUGHTY

563And Jack Daw told us she could not speak!

TRUEWIT

564So it was carried in plot, madam, to put her upon this old fellow, by Sir Dauphine, his nephew, and one or two more of us; but she is a woman of an excellent assurance, and an extraordinary happy wit and tongue. You shall see her make rare sport with Daw ere night.

HAUGHTY

565And he brought us to laugh at her!

TRUEWIT

566That falls out often, madam, that he that thinks himself the master-wit is the master-fool. I assure your ladyship, ye cannot laugh at her.

HAUGHTY

567No, we’ll have her to the college: and she have wit, she shall be one of us! Shall she not, Centaure? We’ll make her a Collegiate.

CENTAURE

568Yes, faith, madam, and Mavis and she will set up a side.

TRUEWIT

569Believe it, madam, and Mistress Mavis, she will sustain her part.

MAVIS

570I’ll tell you that when I have talked with her and tried her.

HAUGHTY

571Use her very civilly, Mavis.

MAVIS

572So I will, madam.

[MAVIS walks apart with EPICENE]

MOROSE

573Blessed minute, that they would whisper thus ever.

TRUEWIT

574In the meantime, madam, would but your ladyship help to vex him a little: you know his disease, talk to him about the wedding-ceremonies, or call for your gloves, or−

HAUGHTY

575Let me alone. Centaure, help me. Master bridegroom, where are you?

MOROSE

576Oh, it was too miraculously good to last!

HAUGHTY

577We see no ensigns of a wedding here, no character of a bridal: where be our scarves and our gloves? I pray you give ’em us. Let’s know your bride’s colours and yours at least.

CENTAURE

578Alas, madam, he has provided none.

MOROSE

579Had I known your ladyship’s painter, I would.

HAUGHTY

580He has given it you, Centaure, i’faith. But do you hear, Master Morose, a jest will not absolve you in this manner. You that have sucked the milk of the court, and from thence have been brought up to the very strong meats and wine of it, been a courtier from the biggin to the nightcap (as we may say), and you to offend in such a high point of ceremony as this, and let your nuptials want all marks of solemnity! How much plate have you lost today (if you had but regarded your profit,) what gifts, what friends, through your mere rusticity?

MOROSE

581Madam−

HAUGHTY

582Pardon me, sir, I must insinuate your errors to you. No gloves? No garters? No scarves? No epithalamium? No masque?

DAW

583Yes, madam, I’ll make an epithalamium, I promise my mistress; I have begun it already: will you ladyship hear it?

HAUGHTY

584Ay, good Jack Daw.

MOROSE

585Will it please your ladyship command a chamber and be private with your friend? You shall have your choice of rooms to retire to after; my whole house is yours. I know it hath been your ladyship’s errand into the city at other times, however now you have been unhappily diverted upon me; but I shall be loath to break any honourable custom of your ladyship’s. And therefore, good madam−

EPICENE

586Come, you are a rude bridegroom, to entertain ladies of honour in this fashion.

CENTAURE

587He is a rude groom indeed.

TRUEWIT

588By that light, you deserve to be grafted, and have your horns reach from one side of the island, to the other−. [To Morose] Do not mistake me, sir; I but speak this to give the ladies some heart again, not for any malice to you.

MOROSE

589Is this your bravo, ladies?

TRUEWIT

590As God help me, if you utter such another word, I’ll take mistress bride in and begin to you in a very sad cup, do you see? Go to, know your friends and such as love you.

ACT 3 SCENE 7

[Enter] CLERIMONT [with MUSICIANS]

CLERIMONT

591By your leave, ladies. Do you want any music? I have brought you variety of noises. Play, sirs, all of you.

Music of all sorts.

MOROSE

592Oh, a plot, a plot, a plot, a plot upon me! This day I shall be their anvil to work on, they will grate me asunder. ’Tis worse than the noise of a saw.

CLERIMONT

593No, they are hair, rosin, and guts. I can give you the receipt.

TRUEWIT

594Peace, boys!

CLERIMONT

595Play, I say.

TRUEWIT

596Peace, rascals! [To Morose] You see who’s your friend now, sir? Take courage, put on a martyr’s resolution. Mock down all their attemptings with patience. ’Tis but a day, and I would suffer heroically. Should an ass exceed me in fortitude? No. You betray your infirmity with your hanging dull ears, and make them insult: bear up bravely and constantly. Look you here, sir, what honour [LA FOOLE passes over sewing the meat with SERVANTS, followed by MISSTRES OTTER] is done you unexpected by your nephew: a wedding-dinner come, and a knight-sewer before it, for the more reputation; and fine Mistress Otter, your neighbour, in the rump or tail of it.

MOROSE

597Is that Gorgon, that Medusa come? Hide me, hide me!

TRUEWIT

598I warrant you, sir, she will not transform you. Look upon her with a good courage. Pray you entertain her and conduct your guests in. No?−Mistress bride, will you entreat in the ladies? Your bridegroom is so shamefaced here−

EPICENE

599Will it please your ladyship, madam?

HAUGHTY

600With the benefit of your company, mistress.

EPICENE

601Servant, pray you perform your duties.

DAW

602And glad to be commanded, mistress.

CENTAURE

603How like you her wit, Mavis?

MAVIS

604Very prettily absolutely well.

MISTRESS OTTER

605[Trying to take precedence] ’Tis my place.

MAVIS

606You shall pardon me, Mistress Otter.

MISTRESS OTTER

607Why, I am a collegiate.

MAVIS

608But not in ordinary.

MISTRESS OTTER

609But I am.

MAVIS

610We’ll dispute that within.

[Exit DAW and the ladies]

CLERIMONT

611Would this had lasted a little longer.

TRUEWIT

612And that they had sent for the heralds. [Enter OTTER.] Captain Otter, what news?

OTTER

613I have brought my bull, bear and horse in private, and yonder are the trumpeters without, and the drum, gentlemen.

The drum and the trumpets sound.

MOROSE

614Oh, Oh, Oh!

OTTER

615And we will have a rouse in each of ’em anon, for bold Britons, i’faith.

[They sound again.]

MOROSE

616Oh, Oh, Oh!

[Exit MOROSE.]

ALL

617Follow, follow, follow!

[Exeunt.]

ACT 4

ACT 4 SCENE I

[Enter] TRUEWIT, CLERIMONT.

TRUEWIT

618Was there ever poor bridegroom so tormented? Or man, indeed?

CLERIMONT

619I have not read of the like in the chronicles of the land.

TRUEWIT

620Sure, he cannot but go to a place of rest after all this purgatory.

CLERIMONT

621He may presume it, I think.

TRUEWIT

622The spitting, the coughing, the laughter, the neezing, the farting, dancing, noise of the music, and her masculine and loud commanding and urging the whole family, makes him think he has married a Fury.

CLERIMONT

623And she carries it up bravely.

TRUEWIT

624Ay, she takes any occasion to speak: that’s the height on’t.

CLERIMONT

625And how soberly Dauphine labours to satisfy him that it was none of his plot!

TRUEWIT

626And has almost brought him to the faith i’the article. [Enter DAUPHINE.] Here he comes.−Where is he now? What’s become of him, Dauphine?

DAUPHINE

627Oh, hold me up a little, I shall go away i’the jest else. He has got on his whole nest of nightcaps, and locked himself up i’the top o’the house, as high as ever he can climb from the noise. I peeped in at a cranny and saw him sitting over a cross-beam o’the roof, like him o’ the saddler’s horse in Fleet Street, upright; and he will sleep there.

CLERIMONT

628But where are your Collegiates?

DAUPHINE

629Withdrawn with the bride in private.

TRUEWIT

630Oh, they are instructing her i’the college grammar. If she have grace with them, she knows all their secrets instantly.

CLERIMONT

631Methinks the lady Haughty looks well today, for all my dispraise of her in the morning. I think, I shall come about to thee again, Truewit.

TRUEWIT

632Believe it, I told you right. Women ought to repair the losses time and years have made i’ their features with dressings. And an intelligent woman, if she know by herself the least defect, will be most curious to hide it; and it becomes her. If she be short, let her sit much, lest when she stands she be thought to sit. If she have an ill foot, let her wear her gown the longer, and her shoe the thinner. If a fat hand and scald nails, let her carve the less, and act in gloves. If a sour breath, let her never discourse fasting, and always talk at her distance. If she have black and rugged teeth, let her offer the less at laughter, especially if she laugh wide and open.

CLERIMONT

633Oh, you shall have some women, when they laugh, you would think they brayed, it is so rude, and−

TRUEWIT

634Ay, and others, that will stalk i’ their gait like an ostrich, and take huge strides. I cannot endure such a sight. I love measure i’n the feet and number i’n the voice: they are gentlenesses that oftentimes draw no less than the face.

DAUPHINE

635How cam’st thou to study these creatures so exactly? I would thou wouldst make me a proficient.

TRUEWIT

636Yes, but you must leave to live i’your chamber, then, a month together upon Amadis de Gaula or Don Quixote, as you are wont, and come abroad where the matter is frequent, to court, to tiltings, public shows and feasts, to plays, and church sometimes: thither they come to show their new tires too, to see, and to be seen. In these places a man shall find whom to love, whom to play with, whom to touch once, whom to hold ever. The variety arrests his judgment. A wench to please a man comes not down dropping from the ceiling, as he lies on his back droning a tobacco-pipe. He must go where she is.

DAUPHINE

637Yes, and be never the near.

TRUEWIT

638Out, heretic! That diffidence makes thee worthy it should be so.

CLERIMONT

639He says true to you, Dauphine.

DAUPHINE

640Why?

TRUEWIT

641A man should not doubt to overcome any woman. Think he can vanquish’em, and he shall; for though they deny, their desire is to be tempted. Penelope herself cannot hold out long. Ostend, you saw, was taken at last. You must persever and hold to your purpose. They would solicit us, but that they are afraid. Howsoever, they wish in their hearts we should solicit them. Praise’em, flatter ’em, you shall never want eloquence or trust; even the chastest delight to feel themselves that way rubbed. With praises you must mix kisses too. If they take them, they’ll take more. Though they strive, they would be overcome.

CLERIMONT

642Oh, but a man must beware of force.

TRUEWIT

643It is to them an acceptable violence, and has ofttimes the place of the greatest courtesy. She that might have been forced, and you let her go free without touching, though then she seem to thank you, will ever hate you after; and glad i’ the face, is assuredly sad at the heart.

CLERIMONT

644But all women are not to be taken all ways.

TRUEWIT

645’Tis true. No more than all birds or all fishes. If you appear learned to an ignorant wench, or jocund to a sad, or witty to a foolish, why, she presently begins to mistrust herself. You must approach them i’ their own height, their own line; for the contrary makes many that fear to commit themselves to noble and worthy fellows run into the embraces of a rascal. If she love wit, give verses, though you borrow ’em of a friend, or buy ’em, to have good. If valour, talk of your sword, and be frequent in the mention of quarrels, though you be staunch in fighting. If activity, be seen o’ your barbary often, or leaping over stools, for the credit of your back. If she love good clothes or dressing, have your learned council about you every morning, your French tailor, barber, linener, etcetera. Let your powder, your glass, and your comb be your dearest acquaintance. Take more care for the ornament of your head than the safety; and wish the commonwealth rather troubled than a hair about you. That will take her. Then, if she be covetous and craving, do you promise anything, and perform sparingly; so shall you keep her in appetite still. Seem as you would give, but be like a barren field that fields little, or unlucky dice to foolish and hoping gamesters. Let your gifts be slight and dainty, rather than precious. Let cunning be above cost. Give cherries at time of year, or apricots; and say they were sent you out o’ the country, though you bought ’em in Cheapside. Admire her tires; like her in all fashions, compare her in every habit to some deity, invent excellent dreams to flatter her, and riddles; or, if she be a great one, perform always the second parts to her: like what she likes, praise whom she praises, and fail not to make the household and servants yours, yea, the whole family, and salute ’em by their names (’tis but light cost if you can purchase ’em so) and make her physician your pensioner, and her chief woman. Nor will it be out of your gain to make love to her too, so she follow, not usher, her lady’s pleasure. All blabbing is taken away when she comes to be a part of the crime.

DAUPHINE

646On what courtly lap hast thou late slept, to come forth so sudden and absolute a courtling?

TRUEWIT

647Good faith, I should rather question you, that are so harkening after these mysteries. I begin to suspect your diligence, Dauphine. Speak, art thou in love in earnest?

DAUPHINE

648Yes, by my troth am I; ’twere ill dissembling before thee.

TRUEWIT

649With which of ’em, I pray you?

DAUPHINE

650With all the Collegiates.

CLERIMONT

651Out on thee. We’ll keep you at home, believe it, i’ the stable, an’ you be such a stallion.

TRUEWIT

652No; I like him well. Men should love wisely, and all women: some one for the face, and let her please the eye; another for the skin, and let her please the touch; and third for the voice, and let her please the ear; and where the objects mix, let the senses so too. Thou wouldst think it strange if I should make ’em all in love with thee afore night!

DAUPHINE

653I would say thou hadst the best philtre i’ the world, and couldst do more than Madam Medea or Doctor Forman.

TRUEWIT

654If I do not, let me play the mountebank for my meat while I live, and the bawd for my drink.

DAUPHINE

655So be it, I say.

ACT 4 SCENE 2

[Enter] OTTER, [with his cups], DAW, and LA FOOLE.

OTTER

656Oh lord, gentlemen, how my knights and I have missed you here!

CLERIMONT

657Why, captain, what service, what service?

OTTER

658To see me bring up my bull, bear and horse to fight.

DAW

659Yes, faith, the captain says we shall be his dogs to bait ’em.

DAUPHINE

660A good employment.

TRUEWIT

661Come on, let’s see a course then.

LA FOOLE

662I am afraid my cousin will be offended if she come.

OTTER

663Be afraid of nothing. Gentlemen, I have placed the drum and the trumpets, and one to give them the sign when you are ready. Here’s my bull for myself, and my bear for Sir John Daw, and my horse for Sir Amorous. Now set your foot to mine, and yours to his, and−

LA FOOLE

664Pray God my cousin come not.

OTTER

665Saint George and Saint Andrew, fear no cousins. Come, sound, sound. Et rauco strepuerunt cornua cantu.

[Drum and trumpets sound. They drink.]

TRUEWIT

666Well said, captain, i’faith; well fought at the bull.

CLERIMONT

667Well held at the bear.

TRUEWIT

668’Loo, ’loo, captain!

DAUPHINE

669Oh, the horse has kicked off his dog already.

LA FOOLE

670I cannot drink it, as I am a knight.

TRUEWIT

671Gods so! Off with his spurs, somebody.

LA FOOLE

672It goes against my conscience. My cousin will be angry with it.

DAW

673I ha’ done mine.

TRUEWIT

674You fought high and fair, Sir John.

CLERIMONT

675At the head.

DAUPHINE

676Like an excellent bear-dog.

CLERIMONT

677[To Daw] You take no notice of the business, I hope.

DAW

678[To Clerimont] Not a word, sir; you see we are jovial.

OTTER

679Sir Amorous, you must not equivocate. It must be pulled down, for all my cousin.

CLERIMONT

680[To La Foole.] ‘Sfoot, if you take not your drink, they’ll think you are discontented with something; you’ll betray all if you take the least notice.

LA FOOLE

681[To Clerimont] Not I; I’ll both drink and talk then.

OTTER

682You must pull the horse on his knees, Sir Amorous. Fear no cousins. Iacta est alea.

TRUEWIT

683[To Dauphine and Clerimont] Oh, now he’s in his vein, and bold. The least hint given him of his wife now will make him rail desperately.

CLERIMONT

684Speak to him of her.

TRUEWIT

685Do you, and I’ll fetch her to the hearing of it.

[Exit.]

DAUPHINE

686Captain he-Otter, your she-Otter is coming, your wife.

OTTER

687Wife! buz! Titivilitium. There’s no such thing in nature. I confess, gentlemen, I have a cook, a laundress, a housedrudge, that serves my necessary turns and goes under that title; but he’s an ass that will be so uxorious to tie his affections to one circle. Come, the name dulls appetite. Here, replenish again: another bout. Wives are nasty sluttish animals.

[He fills the cups again]

DAUPHINE

688Oh, captain!

OTTER

689As ever the earth bare, tribus verbis. Where’s Master Truewit?

DAW

690He’s slipped aside, sir.

CLERIMONT

691But you must drink and be jovial.

DAW

692Yes, give it me.

LA FOOLE

693And me too.

DAW

694Let’s be jovial.

LA FOOLE

695As jovial as you will.

OTTER

696Agreed. Now you shall ha’ the bear, cousin, and Sir John Daw the horse, and I’ll ha’ the bull still. Sound, Tritons o’ the Thames. Nunc est bibendum, nunc pede libero−

[They drink.]MOROSE speaks from above. The trumpets sounding.

MOROSE

697Villains, murderers, sons of the earth and traitors, what do you there?

CLERIMONT

698Oh, now the trumpets have waked him we shall have his company.

OTTER

699A wife is a scurvy clogdogdo, an unlucky thing, a very foresaid bear-whelp, without any good fashion or breeding: mala bestia.

His wife is brought out to hear him [by TRUEWIT].

DAUPHINE

700Why did you marry one then, captain?

OTTER

701A pox!−I married with six thousand pound, I. I was in love with that. I ha’ not kissed my Fury these forty weeks.

CLERIMONT

702The more to blame you, captain.

TRUEWIT

703Nay, Mistress Otter, hear him a little first.

OTTER

704She has a breath worse than my grandmother’s, profecto.

MISTRESS OTTER

705Oh treacherous liar! Kiss me, sweet Master Truewit, and prove him a slandering knave.

TRUEWIT

706I’ll rather believe you, lady.

OTTER

707And she has a peruke that’s like a pound of hemp made up in shoe-threads.

MISTRESS OTTER

708Oh, viper, mandrake!

OTTER

709A most vile face! And yet she spends me forty pound a year in mercury and hogs’ bones. All her teeth were made i’ the Blackfriars, both her eyebrows in the Strand, and her hair in Silver Street. Every part o’ the town owns a piece of her.

MISTRESS OTTER

710I cannot hold.

OTTER

711She takes herself asunder still when she goes to bed, into some twenty boxes, and about next day noon is put together again, like a great German clock; and so comes forth and rings a tedious larum to the whole house, and then is quiet again for an hour, but for her quarters.− Ha’ you done me right, gentlemen?

MISTRESS OTTER

712No, sir, I’ll do you right with my quarters, with my quarters.

She falls upon him ad beats him.

OTTER

713Oh, hold, good princess.

TRUEWIT

714Sound, sound!

[Drum and trumpets sound.]

CLERIMONT

715A battle, a battle!

MISTRESS OTTER

716You notorious stinkardly bearward, does my breath smell?

OTTER

717Under correction, dear princess. Look to my bear and my horse, gentlemen.

MISTRESS OTTER

718Do I want teeth and eyebrows, thou bull-dog?

TRUEWIT

719Sound, sound still.

[They sound again.]

OTTER

720No, I protest, under correction−

MISTRESS OTTER

721Ay, now you are under correction, you protest; but you did not protest before correction, sir. Thou Judas, to offer to betray thy princess! I’ll make thee an example−

MOROSE descends with a long sword.

MOROSE

722I will have no such examples in my house, Lady Otter.

MISTRESS OTTER

723Ah!

[She runs off, with DAW and LA FOOLE.]

MOROSE

724Mistress Mary Ambree, your examples are dangerous.−Rogues, hell-hounds, Stentors, out of my doors, you sons of noise and tumult, begot on an ill May-day, or when the galley-foist is afloat to Westminster! A trumpeter could not be conceived but then!

[Drives out the musicians.]

DAUPHINE

725What ails you, sir?

MOROSE

726They have rent my roof, walls and all my windows asunder, with their brazen throats.

[Exit.]

TRUEWIT

727Best follow him, Dauphine.

DAUPHINE

728So I will.

[Exit.]

CLERIMONT

729Where’s Daw and La Foole?

OTTER

730They are both run away, sir. Good gentlemen, help to pacify my princess, and speak to the great ladies for me. Now must I go lie with the bears this fortnight, and keep out o’ the way till my peace be made, for this scandal she has taken. Did you not see my bull-head, gentlemen?

CLERIMONT

731Is’t not on, captain?

TRUEWIT

732No:− [To Clerimont] but he may make a new one, by that is on.

OTTER

733Oh, here ‘tis. An you come over, gentlemen, and ask for Tom Otter, we’ll go down to Ratcliffe, and have a course i’faith, for all these disasters. There’s bona spes left.

TRUEWIT

734Away, captain, get off while you are well.

[Exit OTTER.]

CLERIMONT

735I am glad we are rid of him.

TRUEWIT

736You had never been, unless we had put his wife upon him. His humour is as tedious at last, as it was ridiculous at first.

ACT 4 SCENE 3

[Enter] HAUGHTY, MISTRESS OTTER, MAVIS, DAW, LA FOOLE, CENTAURE, AND EPICENE. [TRUEWIT and CLERIMONT observe them.]

HAUGHTY

737We wondered why you shrieked so, Mistress Otter.

MISTRESS OTTER

738O God, madam, he came down with a huge long naked weapon in both his hands, and looked so dreadfully! Sure he’s beside himself.

MAVIS

739Why, what made you there, Mistress Otter?

MISTRESS OTTER

740Alas, Mistress Mavis, I was chastising my subject, and thought nothing of him.

DAW

741[To Epicene] Faith, mistress, you must do so too. Learn to chastise. Mistress Otter corrects her husband so, he dares not speak but under correction.

LA FOOLE

742And with his hat off to her: ’twould do you good to see.

HAUGHTY

743In sadness, ’tis good and mature counsel: practice it, Morose. I’ll call you Morose still now, as I call Centaure and Mavis: we four will be all one.

CENTAURE

744And you’ll come to the college and live with us?

HAUGHTY

745Make him give milk and honey.

MAVIS

746Look how you manage him at first, you shall have him ever after.

CENTAURE

747Let him allow you your coach and four horses, your woman, your chambermaid, your page, your gentleman-usher, your French cook, and four grooms.

HAUGHTY

748And go with us to Bedlam, to the china-houses, and to the Exchange.

CENTAURE

749It will open the gate to your fame.

HAUGHTY

750Here’s Centaure has immortalised herself with taming of her wild male.

MAVIS

751Ay, she has done the miracle of the kingdom.

EPICENE

752But ladies, do you count it lawful to have such plurality of servants, and do ’em all graces?

HAUGHTY

753Why not? Why should women deny their favours to men? Are they the poorer, or the worse?

DAW

754Is the Thames the less for the dyer’s water, mistress?

LA FOOLE

755Or a torch for lighting many torches?

TRUEWIT

756[Aside] Well said, La Foole; what a new one he has got!

CENTAURE

757They are empty losses women fear in this kind.

HAUGHTY

758Besides, ladies should be mindful of the approach of age, and let no time want his due use. The best of our days pass first.

MAVIS

759We are rivers, that cannot be called back, madam: she that now excludes her lovers may live to lie a forsaken beldame in a frozen bed.

CENTAURE

760’Tis true, Mavis. And who will wait on us to coach then? or write, or tell us the news then? Make anagrams of our names, and invite us to the cockpit, and kiss our hands all the play-time, and draw their weapons for our honours?

HAUGHTY

761Not one.

DAW

762Nay, my mistress is not altogether unintelligent of these things; here be in presence have tasted of her favours.

TRUEWIT

763[Aside] What a neighing hobby-horse is this!

EPICENE

764But not with intent to boast ’em again, servant. And have you those excellent receipts, madam, to keep yourselves from bearing of children?

HAUGHTY

765Oh yes, Morose. How should we maintain our youth and beauty else? Many births of a woman make her old, as many crops make the earth barren.

ACT 4 SCENE 4

[Enter] MOROSE and DAUPHINE [who speak apart.]

MOROSE

766Oh my cursed angel, that instructed me to this fate!

DAUPHINE

767Why, sir?

MOROSE

768That I should be seduced by so foolish a devil as a barber will make!

DAUPHINE

769I would I had been worthy, sir, to have partaken your counsel; you should never have trusted it to such a minister.

MOROSE

770Would I could redeem it with the loss of an eye (nephew), a hand, or any other member.

DAUPHINE

771Marry, God forbid, sir, that you should geld yourself to anger your wife.

MOROSE

772So it would rid me of her! And that I did supererogatory penance, in a belfry, at Westminster Hall, i’n the Cockpit, at the fall of a stag, the Tower Wharf (what place is there else?) London Bridge, Paris Garden, Billinsgate, when the noises are at their height and loudest. Nay, I would sit out a play, that were nothing but fights at sea, drum, trumpet and target!

DAUPHINE

773I hope there shall be no such need, sir. Take patience, good uncle. This is but a day, and ’tis well worn too now.

MOROSE

774O, ’twill be so for ever, nephew, I foresee it, for ever. Strife and tumult are the dowry that comes with a wife.

TRUEWIT

775I told you so, sir, and you would not believe me.

MOROSE

776Alas, do not rub those wounds, Master Truewit, to blood again; ’twas my negligence. Add not affliction to affliction. I have perceived the effect of it, too late, in Madam Otter.

EPICENE

777[Coming forward] How do you, sir?

MOROSE

778Did you ever hear a more unnecessary question? As if she did not see! Why, I do as you see, empress, empress.

EPICENE

779You are not well, sir! You look very ill! Something has distempered you.

MOROSE

780Oh horrible, monstrous impertinencies! Would not one of these have served? Do you think, sir? Would not one of these have served?

TRUEWIT

781Yes, sir, but these are but notes of female kindness, sir; certain tokens that she has a voice, sir.

MOROSE

782Oh, is’t so? Come, and’t be no otherwise−what say you?

EPICENE

783How do you feel yourself, sir?

MOROSE

784Again that!

TRUEWIT

785Nay, look you, sir: you would be friends with your wife upon unconscionable terms, her silence−

EPICENE

786They say you are run mad, sir.

MOROSE

787Not for love, I assure you, of you; do you see?

EPICENE

788O lord, gentlemen! Lay hold on him for God’s sake. What shall I do? Who’s his physician (can you tell) that knows the state of his body best, that I might send for him? Good sir, speak. I’ll send for one of my doctors else.

MOROSE

789What, to poison me, that I might die intestate and leave you possessed of all?

EPICENE

790Lord, how idly he talks, and how his eyes sparkle! He looks green about the temples! Do you see what blue spots he has?

TRUEWIT

791Ay, it’s melancholy.

EPICENE

792Gentlemen, for heaven’s sake counsel me. Ladies! Servant, you have read Pliny and Paracelsus: ne’er a word now to comfort a poor gentlewoman? Ay me! What fortune had I to marry a distracted man?

DAW

793I’ll tell you, mistress−

TRUEWIT

794[To Clerimont] How rarely she holds it up!

[TRUEWIT and CLERIMONT prevent MOROSE from leaving.]

MOROSE

795What mean you, gentlemen?

EPICENE

796What will you tell me, servant?

DAW

797The disease in Greek is called mania, in Latin insania, furor, vel ecstasis melancholica, that is, egressio, when a man ex melancholico evadit fanaticus.

MOROSE

798Shall I have a lecture read upon me alive?

DAW

799But he may be but phreneticus yet, mistress, and phrenetis is only delirium, or so−

EPICENE

800Ay, that is for the disease, servant; but what is this to the cure? We are sure enough of the disease.

MOROSE

801Let me go!

TRUEWIT

802Why, we’ll entreat her to hold her peace, sir.

MOROSE

803Oh no, labour not to stop her. She is like a conduit-pipe, that will gush out with more force when she opens again.

HAUGHTY

804I’ll tell you, Morose, you must talk divinity to him altogether, or moral philosophy.

LA FOOLE

805Ay, and there’s an excellent book of moral philosophy, madam, of Reynard the Fox and all the beasts, called Doni’s Philosophy.

CENTAURE

806There is, indeed, sir Amorous La Foole.

MOROSE

807Oh misery!

LA FOOLE

808I have read it, my lady Centaure, all over to my cousin, here.

MISTRESS OTTER

809Ay, and ’tis a very good book as any is of the moderns.

DAW

810Tut, he must have Seneca read to him, and Plutarch and the ancients; the moderns are not for this disease.

CLERIMONT

811Why, you discommended them too, today, Sir John.

DAW

812Ay, in some cases; but in these they are best, and Aristotle’s Ethics.

MAVIS

813Say you so sir John? I think you are deceived: you took it upon trust.

HAUGHTY

814Where’s Trusty, my woman? I’ll end this difference. I prithee, Otter, call her. Her father and mother were both mad when they put her to me.

[Exit MISTRESS OTTER.]

MOROSE

815I think so.−Nay, gentlemen, I am tame. This is but an exercise, I know, a marriage ceremony, which I must endure.

HAUGHTY

816And one of ’em (I know not which) was cured with the Sick Man’s Salve; and the other with Greene’s Groat’s-worth of Wit.

TRUEWIT

817A very cheap cure, madam.

HAUGHTY

818Ay, it’s very feasible.

[Enter MISTRESS OTTER with TRUSTY.]

MISTRESS OTTER

819My lady called for you, Mistress Trusty; you must decide a controversy.

HAUGHTY

820Oh, Trusty, which was it you said, your father or your mother, that was cured with the Sick Man’s Salve?

TRUSTY

821My mother, madam, with the Salve.

TRUEWIT

822Then it was The sick woman’s Salve?

TRUSTY

823And my father with The Groat’s-worth of Wit. But there was other means used: we had a preacher that would preach folk asleep still; and so they were prescribed to go to church by an old woman that was their physician, thrice a week−

EPICENE

824To sleep?

TRUSTY

825Yes, forsooth; and every night they read themselves asleep on those books.

EPICENE

826Good faith, it stands with great reason. I would I knew where to procure those books.

MOROSE

827Oh!

LA FOOLE

828I can help you with one of ’em, Mistress Morose, the Groat’s-worth of Wit.

EPICENE

829But I shall disfurnish you, Sir Amorous. Can you spare it?

LA FOOLE

830Oh, yes, for a week or so; I’ll read it myself to him.

EPICENE

831No, I must do that, sir; that must be my office.

MOROSE

832Oh, oh!

EPICENE

833Sure, he would do well enough, if he could sleep.

MOROSE

834No, I should do well enough, if you could sleep. Have I no friend that will make her drunk? or give her a little laudanum? or opium?

TRUEWIT

835Why, sir, she talks ten times worse in her sleep.

MOROSE

836How!

CLERIMONT

837Do you not know that, sir? Never ceases all night.

TRUEWIT

838And snores like a porpoise.

MOROSE

839Oh, redeem me, fate, redeem me, fate! For how many causes may a man be divorced, nephew?

DAUPHINE

840I know not, truly, sir.

TRUEWIT

841Some divine must resolve you in that, sir, or canon lawyer.

MOROSE

842I will not rest, I will not think of any other hope or comfort, till I know.

[Exit MOROSE and DAUPHINE.]

CLERIMONT

843Alas, poor man!

TRUEWIT

844You’ll make him mad indeed, ladies, if you pursue this.

HAUGHTY

845No, we’ll let him breathe now a quarter of an hour or so.

CLERIMONT

846By my faith, a large truce.

HAUGHTY

847Is that his keeper that is gone with him?

DAW

848It is his nephew, madam.

LA FOOLE

849Sir Dauphine Eugenie.

HAUGHTY

850He looks like a very pitiful knight−

DAW

851As can be. This marriage has put him out of all.

LA FOOLE

852He has not a penny in his purse, madam−

DAW

853He is ready to cry all this day.

LA FOOLE

854A very shark, he set me i’n the nick t’other night at primero.

TRUEWIT

855[To Clerimont] How these swabbers talk!

CLERIMONT

856[To Truewit] Ay, Otter’s wine has swelled their humours above a spring-tide.

HAUGHTY

857Good Morose, let’s go in again. I like your couches exceeding well; we’ll go lie and talk there.

EPICENE

858I wait on you, madam.

[Exeunt HAUGHTY, CENTAURE, MAVIS, TRUSTY, DAW and LA FOOLE.]

TRUEWIT

859’Slight, I will have ’em as silent as signs, and their posts too, ere I ha’ done. Do you hear, lady bride? I pray thee now, as thou art a noble wench, continue this discourse of Dauphine within; but praise him exceedingly. Magnify him with all the height of affection thou canst (I have some purpose in’t) and but beat off these two rooks, Jack Daw and his fellow, with any discontentment hither, and I’ll honour thee for ever.

EPICENE

860I was about it here. It angered me to the soul to hear ’em begin to talk so malapert.

TRUEWIT

861Pray thee perform it, and thou win’st me an idolater to thee everlasting.

EPICENE

862Will you go in and hear me do it?

TRUEWIT

863No, I’ll stay here. Drive ’em out of your company, ’tis all I ask; which cannot be any way better done than by extolling Dauphine, whom they have so slighted.

EPICENE

864I warrant you; you shall expect one of ’em presently.

[Exit.]

CLERIMONT

865What a cast of kestrils are these, to hawk after ladies thus!

TRUEWIT

866Ay, and strike at such an eagle as Dauphine.

CLERIMONT

867He will be mad when we tell him. Here he comes.

ACT 4 SCENE 5

[Enter] DAUPHINE.

CLERIMONT

868O sir, you are welcome.

TRUEWIT

869Where’s thine uncle?

DAUPHINE

870Run out o’ doors in’s nightcaps to talk with a casuist about his divorce. It works admirably.

TRUEWIT

871Thou wouldst ha’ said so if thou hadst been here! The ladies have laughed at thee most comically since thou went’st, Dauphine.

CLERIMONT

872And asked if thou wert thine uncle’s keeper.

TRUEWIT

873And the brace of baboons answered yes, and said thou wert a pitiful poor fellow and didst live upon posts, and hadst nothing but three suits of apparel and some few benevolences that lords ga’ thee to fool to ’em and swagger.

DAUPHINE

874Let me not live, I’ll beat them. I’ll bind ’em both to grand madam’s bed-posts and have ’em baited with monkeys.

TRUEWIT

875Thou shalt not need, they shall be beaten to thy hand, Dauphine. I have an execution to serve upon ’em I warrant thee shall serve: trust my plot.

DAUPHINE

876Ay, you have many plots! So you had one to make all the wenches in love with me.

TRUEWIT

877Why, if I do not yet afore night, as near as ’tis, and that they do not every one invite thee and be ready to scratch for thee, take the mortgage of my wit.

CLERIMONT

878’Fore God, I’ll be his witness; thou shalt have it, Dauphine; thou shalt be his fool for ever if thou dost not.

TRUEWIT

879Agreed. Perhaps ’twill be the better estate. Do you observe this gallery, or rather lobby, indeed? Here are a couple of studies, at each end one: here will I act such a tragicomedy between the Guelphs and the Ghibellines, Daw and La Foole. Which of ’em comes out first will I seize on. (You two shall be the chorus behind the arras, and whip out between the acts and speak.) If I do not make ’em keep the peace for this remnant of the day, if not of the year, I have failed once−I hear Daw coming. Hide, and do not laugh, for God’s sake.

[DAUPHINE and CLERIMONT hide.]

[Enter DAW.]

DAW

880Which is the way into the garden, trow?

TRUEWIT

881Oh, Jack Daw! I am glad I have met with you. In good faith, I must have this matter go no further between you. I must ha’ it taken up.

DAW

882What matter, sir? Between whom?

TRUEWIT

883Come, you disguise it: Sir Amorous and you. If you love me, Jack, you shall make use of your philosophy now, for this once, and deliver me your sword. This is not the wedding the Centaurs were at, though there be a she-one here. The bride has entreated me I will see no blood shed at her bridal; you saw her whisper me erewhile.

[Takes his sword.]

DAW

884As I hope to finish Tacitus, I intend no murder.

TRUEWIT

885Do you not wait for Sir Amorous?

DAW

886Not I, by my knighthood.

TRUEWIT

887And your scholarship too?

DAW

888And my scholarship too.

TRUEWIT

889Go to, then I return you your sword, and ask you mercy; but put it not up, for you will be assaulted. [Returns his sword.] I understood that you had apprehended it, and walked here to brave him, and that you had held your life contemptible in regard of your honour.

DAW

890No, no, no such thing, I assure you. He and I parted now as good friends as could be.

TRUEWIT

891Trust not you to that visor. I saw him since dinner with another face. I have known many men in my time vexed with losses, with deaths, and with abuses, but so offended a wight as Sir Amorous did I never see, or read of. For taking away his guests, sir, today, that’s the cause, and he declares it behind your back with such threat’nings and contempts. He said to Dauphine you were the arrant’st ass−

DAW

892Ay, he may say his pleasure.

TRUEWIT

893And swears you are so protested a coward that he knows you will never do him any manly or single right, and therefore he will take his course.

DAW

894I’ll give him any satisfaction, sir−but fighting.

TRUEWIT

895Ay, sir, but who knows what satisfaction he’ll take? Blood he thirsts for, and blood he will have; and whereabouts on you he will have it, who knows but himself?

DAW

896I pray you, Master Truewit, be you a mediator.

TRUEWIT

897Well, sir, conceal yourself then in this study till I return. [He puts him up.] Nay, you must be content to be locked in; for, for mine own reputation, I would not have you seen to receive a public disgrace, while I have the matter in managing. Gods so, here he comes! Keep your breath close, that he do not hear you sigh.−In good faith, Sir Amorous, he is not this way; I pray you be merciful, do not murder him; he is a Christian as good as you; you are armed as if you sought a revenge on all his race. Good Dauphine, get him away from this place. I never knew a man’s choler so high but he would speak to his friends, he would hear reason.−Jack Daw, Jack Daw! Asleep!

DAW

898[Within] Is he gone, Master Truewit?

TRUEWIT

899Ay, did you hear him?

DAW

900Oh God! yes.

TRUEWIT

901[Aside] What a quick ear fear has!

DAW

902[Comes out of the study.] But is he so armed, as you say?

TRUEWIT

903Armed? Did you ever see a fellow set out to take possession?

DAW

904Ay, sir.

TRUEWIT

905That may give you some light to conceive of him; but ’tis nothing to the principal. Some false brother i’ the house has furnished him strangely. Or, if it were out o’ the house, it was Tom Otter.

DAW

906Indeed he’s a captain, and his wife is his kinswoman.

TRUEWIT

907He has got somebody’s old two-hand sword, to mow you off at the knees. And that sword hath spawned such a dagger!− But then he is so hung with pikes, halberds, petronels, calivers and muskets, that he looks like a justice of peace’s hall; a man of two thousand a year, is not cessed at so many weapons as he has on. There was never fencer challenged at so many several foils. You would think he meant to murder all Saint Pulchre’s parish. If he could but victual himself for half a year in his breeches, he is sufficiently armed to overrun a country.

DAW

908Good lord! what means he, sir? I pray you, Master Truewit, be you a mediator.

TRUEWIT

909Well, I’ll try if he will be appeased with a leg or an arm; if not, you must die once.

DAW

910I would be loath to lose my right arm, for writing madrigals.

TRUEWIT

911Why, if he will be satisfied with a thumb or a little finger, all’s one to me. You must think I’ll do my best.

DAW

912Good sir, do.

He puts him up again, and then [DAUPHINE and CLERIMONT] come forth.]

CLERIMONT

913What hast thou done?

TRUEWIT

914He will let me do nothing, man, he does all afore me; he offers his left arm.

CLERIMONT

915His left wing for a Jack Daw.

DAUPHINE

916Take it by all means.

TRUEWIT

917How! Maim a man for ever for a jest? What a conscience hast thou?

DAUPHINE

918’Tis no loss to him: he has no employment for his arms but to eat spoon-meat. Beside, as good maim his body as his reputation.

TRUEWIT

919He is a scholar and a Wit, and yet he does not think so. But he loses no reputation with us, for we all resolved him an ass before. To your places again.

CLERIMONT

920I pray thee let me be in at the other a little.

TRUEWIT

921Look, you’ll spoil all: these be ever your tricks.

CLERIMONT

922No, but I could hit of some things that thou wilt miss, and thou wilt say are good ones.

TRUEWIT

923I warrant you. I pray forbear, I will leave it off else.

DAUPHINE

924Come away, Clerimont.

[They hide again.]

[Enter LA FOOLE.]

TRUEWIT

925Sir Amorous!

LA FOOLE

926Master Truewit.

TRUEWIT

927Whither were you going?

LA FOOLE

928Down into the court to make water.

TRUEWIT

929By no means, sir; you shall rather tempt your breeches.

LA FOOLE

930Why, sir?

TRUEWIT

931[Opening the door of the other study.] Enter here if you love your life.

LA FOOLE

932Why? Why?

TRUEWIT

933Question till your throat be cut, do; dally till the enraged soul find you.

LA FOOLE

934Who’s that?

TRUEWIT

935Daw it is; will you in?

LA FOOLE

936Ay, ay, I’ll in; what’s the matter?

TRUEWIT

937Nay, if he had been cool enough to tell us that, there had been some hope to atone you, but he seems so implacably enraged!

LA FOOLE

938’Slight, let him rage! I’ll hide myself.

TRUEWIT

939Do, good sir. But what have you done to him within that should provoke him thus? You have broke some jest upon him afore the ladies−

LA FOOLE

940Not I, never in my life broke jest upon any man. The bride was praising Sir Dauphine, and he went away in snuff, and I followed him, unless he took offence at me in his drink erewhile, that I would not pledge all the horse-full.

TRUEWIT

941By my faith, and that may be, you remember well; but he walks the round up and down, through every room o’ the house, with a towel in his hand, crying, ‘Where’s La Foole? Who saw La−Foole?’ And when Dauphine and I demanded the cause, we can force no answer from him but ‘O revenge, how sweet art thou! I will strangle him in this towel’−which leads us to conjecture that the main cause of his fury is for bringing your meat today, with a towel about you, to his discredit.

LA FOOLE

942Like enough. Why, and he be angry for that, I’ll stay here till his anger be blown over.

TRUEWIT

943A good becoming resolution, sir. If you can put it on o’ the sudden.

LA FOOLE

944Yes, I can put it on. Or I’ll away into the country presently.

TRUEWIT

945How will you get out o’the house, sir? He knows you are i’the house, and he’ll watch you this se’en-night but he’ll have you. He’ll outwait a sergeant for you.

LA FOOLE

946Why then I’ll stay here.

TRUEWIT

947You must think how to victual yourself in time then.

LA FOOLE

948Why, sweet Master Truewit, will you entreat my cousin Otter to send me a cold venison pasty, a bottle or two of wine, and a chamber-pot?

TRUEWIT

949A stool were better, sir, of Sir A-jax his invention.

LA FOOLE

950Ay, that will be better indeed; and a pallet to lie on.

TRUEWIT

951Oh, I would not advise you to sleep by any means.

LA FOOLE

952Would you not, sir? Why then I will not.

TRUEWIT

953Yet there’s another fear−

LA FOOLE

954Is there, sir? What is’t?

TRUEWIT

955No, he cannot break open this door with his foot, sure.

LA FOOLE

956I’ll set my back against it, sir. I have a good back.

TRUEWIT

957But then if he should batter.

LA FOOLE

958Batter! If he dare, I’ll have an action of batt’ry against him.

TRUEWIT

959Cast you the worst. He has sent for powder already, and what he will do with it, no man knows; perhaps blow up the corner o’ the house where he suspects you are. Here he comes! In, quickly. [He feigns as if one were present, to fight the other, who is run in to hide himself.] I protest, Sir John Daw, he is not this way. What will you do? Before God, you shall hang no petard here. I’ll die rather. Will you not take my word? I never knew one but would be satisfied.−Sir Amorous, there’s no standing out. He has made a petard of an old brass pot, to force your door. Think upon some satisfaction or terms to offer him.

LA FOOLE

960[Within.] Sir, I’ll give him any satisfaction. I dare give any terms.

TRUEWIT

961You’ll leave it to me then?

LA FOOLE

962Ay, sir. I’ll stand to any conditions.

[TRUEWIT] calls forth CLERIMONT and DAUPHINE.

TRUEWIT

963How now, what think you, sirs? Were’t not a difficult thing to determine which of these two feared most?

CLERIMONT

964Yes, but this fears the bravest; the other a whiniling dastard, Jack Daw. But La Foole, a brave heroic coward! And is afraid in a great look and a stout accent. I like him rarely.

TRUEWIT

965Had it not been pity these two should ha’ been concealed?

CLERIMONT

966Shall I make a motion?

TRUEWIT

967Briefly. For I must strike while ’tis hot.

CLERIMONT

968Shall I go fetch the ladies to the catastrophe?

TRUEWIT

969Umh? Ay, by my troth.

DAUPHINE

970By no mortal means. Let them continue in the state of ignorance, and err still; think ’em wits and fine fellows as they have done. ’Twere sin to reform them.

TRUEWIT

971Well, I will have ‘em fetched, now I think on’t, for a private purpose of mine; do, Clerimont, fetch ’em, and discourse to ’em all that’s past, and bring ’em into the gallery here.

DAUPHINE

972This is thy extreme vanity now; thou think’st thou wert undone if every jest thou mak’st were not published.

TRUEWIT

973Thou shalt see how unjust thou art presently. Clerimont, say it was Dauphine’s plot. [Exit CLERIMONT.] Trust me not if the whole drift be not for thy good. There’s a carpet i’ the next room; put it on, with this scarf over thy face and a cushion o’ thy head, and be ready when I call Amorous. Away− [Exit DAUPHINE.] John Daw!

[He brings DAW out of his study.]

DAW

974What good news, sir?

TRUEWIT

975Faith, I have followed and argued with him hard for you. I told him you were a knight and a scholar, and that you knew fortitude did consist magis patiendo quam faciendo, magis ferendo quam feriendo.

DAW

976It doth so indeed, sir.

TRUEWIT

977And that you would suffer, I told him: so at first he demanded, by my troth, in my conceit too much.

DAW

978What was it, sir?

TRUEWIT

979Your upper lip, and six o’ your fore-teeth.

DAW

980’Twas unreasonable.

TRUEWIT

981Nay, I told him plainly, you could not spare ’em all. So after long argument (pro et con, as you know) I brought him down to your two butter-teeth, and them he would have.

DAW

982Oh, did you so? Why, he shall have ’em.

[Enter above HAUGHTY, CENTAURE, MAVIS, MISTRESS OTTER, EPICENE, TRUSTY and CLERIMONT]

TRUEWIT

983But he shall not, sir, by your leave. The conclusion is this, sir: because you shall be very good friends hereafter, and this never to be remembered or upbraided, besides that he may not boast he has done any such thing to you in his own person, he is to come here in disguise, give you five kicks in private, sir, take your sword from you, and lock you up in that study, during pleasure. Which will be but a little while, we’ll get it released presently.

DAW

984Five kicks? He shall have six, sir, to be friends.

TRUEWIT

985Believe me, you shall not overshoot yourself to send him that word by me.

DAW

986Deliver it, sir. He shall have it with all my heart, to be friends.

TRUEWIT

987Friends? Nay, and he should not be so, and heartily too, upon these terms, he shall have me to enemy while I live. Come, sir, bear it bravely.

DAW

988O God, sir, ’tis nothing.

TRUEWIT

989True. Whats’s six kicks to a man that reads Seneca?

DAW

990I have had a hundred, sir.

TRUEWIT

991Sir Amorous! No speaking one to another, or rehearsing old matters.

DAUPHINE [masked with a scarf] comes forth and kicks him.

DAW

992One, two, three, four, five. I protest, Sir Amorous, you shall have six.

TRUEWIT

993Nay, I told you, you should not talk. Come, give him six, an he will needs. [DAUPHINE kicks him again.] Your swords. [DAW gives TRUEWIT his sword.] Now return to your safe custody: you shall presently meet afore the ladies, and be the dearest friends one to another− [DAW goes into his study.] Give me the scarf; now thou shalt beat the other barefaced. Stand by, Sir Amorous!

[Exit DAUPHINE; TRUEWIT brings out LA FOOLE.]

LA FOOLE

994What’s here? A sword!

TRUEWIT

995I cannot help it, without I should take the quarrel upon myself; here he has sent you his sword−

LA FOOLE

996I’ll receive none on’t.

TRUEWIT

997And he wills you to fasten it against a wall, and break your head in some few several places against the hilts.

LA FOOLE

998I will not: tell him roundly. I cannot endure to shed my own blood.

TRUEWIT

999Will you not?

LA FOOLE

1000No. I’ll beat it against a fair flat wall, if that will satisfy him; if not, he shall beat it himself for Amorous.

TRUEWIT

1001Why, this is strange starting off, when a man undertakes for you! I offered him another condition: will you stand to that?

LA FOOLE

1002Ay, what is’t?

TRUEWIT

1003That you will be beaten in private.

LA FOOLE

1004Yes. I am content, at the blunt.

TRUEWIT

1005Then you must submit yourself to be hoodwinked in this scarf, and be led to him, where he will take your sword from you, and make you bear a blow over the mouth, gules, and tweaks by the nose sans nombre.

LA FOOLE

1006I am content. But why must I be blinded?

TRUEWIT

1007That’s for your good, sir: because, if he should grow insolent upon this and publish it hereafter to your disgrace (which I hope he will not do) you might swear safely and protest he never beat you, to your knowledge.

LA FOOLE

1008Oh, I conceive.

TRUEWIT

1009I do not doubt but you’ll be perfect good friends upon’t, and not dare to utter an ill thought one of another in future.

LA FOOLE

1010Not I, as God help me, of him.

TRUEWIT

1011Nor he of you, sir. If he should−Come, sir. [Blindfolds him.] All hid, Sir John.

DAUPHINE enters to tweak him.

LA FOOLE

1012Oh, Sir John, Sir John! Oh, Oh, Oh, Oh, Oh, Oh, Oh−

[DAUPHINE takes his sword.]

TRUEWIT

1013Good, Sir John, leave tweaking, you’ll blow his nose off. [Exit DAUPHINE with the two swords.] ’Tis Sir John’s pleasure you should retire into the study. [Unbinds LA FOOLE’s eyes and shuts him in.] Why, now you are friends. All bitterness between you, I hope, is buried; you shall come forth by and by Damon and Pythias upon’t, and embrace with all the rankness of friendship that can be. [Enter DAUPHINE.] I trust we shall have ’em tamer i’their language hereafter. Dauphine, I worship thee.−God’s will, the ladies have surprised us!

ACT 4 SCENCE 6

[Enter] HAUGHTY, CENTAURE, MAVIS, MISTRESS OTTER, EPICENE, TRUSTY [and CLERIMONT], having discovered part of the past scene, above.

HAUGHTY

1014Centaure, how our judgments were imposed on by these adulterate knights!

CENTAURE

1015Nay, madam, Mavis was more deceived than we; ’twas her commendation uttered ’em in the college.

MAVIS

1016I commended but their wits, madam, and their braveries. I never looked toward their valours.

HAUGHTY

1017Sir Dauphine is valiant, and a Wit too, it seems.

MAVIS

1018And a Bravery too.

HAUGHTY

1019Was this his project?

MISTRESS OTTER

1020So Master Clerimont intimates, madam.

HAUGHTY

1021Good Morose, when you come to the college, will you bring him with you? He seems a very perfect gentleman.

EPICENE

1022He is so, madam, believe it.

CENTAURE

1023But when will you come, Morose?

EPICENE

1024Three or four days hence, madam, when I have got me a coach and horses.

HAUGHTY

1025No, tomorrow, good Morose; Centaure shall send you her coach.

MAVIS

1026Yes, faith, do, and bring Sir Dauphine with you.

HAUGHTY

1027She has promised that, Mavis.

MAVIS

1028He is a very worthy gentleman in his exteriors, madam.

HAUGHTY

1029Ay, he shows he is judicial in his clothes.

CENTAURE

1030And yet not so superlatively neat as some, madam, that have their faces set in a brake!

HAUGHTY

1031Ay, and have every hair in form!

MAVIS

1032That wear purer linen then ourselves, and profess more neatness than the French hermaphrodite!

EPICENE

1033Ay, ladies, they; what they tell one of us, have told a thousand, and are the only thieves of our fame; that think to take us with that perfume or with that lace, and laugh at us unconscionably when they have done.

HAUGHTY

1034But Sir Dauphine’s carelessness becomes him.

CENTAURE

1035I could love a man for such a nose!

MAVIS

1036Or such a leg!

CENTAURE

1037He has an exceeding good eye, madam!

MAVIS

1038And a very good lock!

CENTAURE

1039Good Morose, bring him to my chamber first.

MISTRESS OTTER

1040Please your honours to meet at my house, madam?

TRUEWIT

1041[To Dauphine] See how they eye thee, man! They are taken, I warrant thee.

HAUGHTY

1042[Approaching Truewit and Dauphine.] You have unbraced our brace of knights here, Master Truewit.

TRUEWIT

1043Not I, madam, it was sir Dauphine’s engine; who, if he have disfurnished your ladyship of any guard or service by it, is able to make the place good again in himself.

HAUGHTY

1044There’s no suspicion of that, sir.

CENTAURE

1045God so, Mavis, Haughty is kissing.

MAVIS

1046Let us go too and take part.

HAUGHTY

1047But I am glad of the fortune (beside the discovery of two such empty caskets) to gain the knowledge of so rich a mine of virtue as Sir Dauphine.

CENTAURE

1048We would be all glad to style him of our friendship, and see him at the college.

MAVIS

1049He cannot mix with a sweeter society, I’ll prophesy, and I hope he himself will think so.

DAUPHINE

1050I should be rude to imagine otherwise, lady.

TRUEWIT

1051[To Dauphine] Did not I tell thee, Dauphine? Why, all their actions are governed by crude opinion, without reason or cause; they know not why they do anything; but as they are informed, believe, judge, praise, condemn, love, hate, and in emulation one of another, do all these things alike. Only, they have a natural inclination sways ’em generally to the worst, when they are left to themselves. But pursue it, now thou hast ’em.

HAUGHTY

1052Shall we go in again, Morose?

EPICENE

1053Yes, madam.

CENTAURE

1054We’ll entreat Sir Dauphine’s company.

TRUEWIT

1055Stay, good madam, the interview of the two friends, Pylades and Orestes: I’ll fetch ‘em out to you straight.

HAUGHTY

1056Will you, Master Truewit?

DAUPHINE

1057Ay, but noble ladies, do not confess in your countenance or outward bearing to ’em any discovery of their follies, that we may see how they will bear up again, with what assurance and erection.

HAUGHTY

1058We will not, Sir Dauphine.

CENTAURE [and] MAVIS

1059Upon our honours, Sir Dauphine.

TRUEWIT

1060Sir Amorous, Sir Amorous! The ladies are here.

LA FOOLE

1061[Within] Are they?

TRUEWIT

1062Yes, but slip out by and by as their backs are turned and meet Sir John here, as by chance, when I call you.− Jack Daw!

DAW

1063[Within] What say you, sir?

TRUEWIT

1064Whip out behind me suddenly, and no anger i’ your looks to your adversary.−Now, now!

[LA FOOLE and DAW come out of their studies, and salute each other.]

LA FOOLE

1065Noble Sir John Daw! Where ha’ you been?

DAW

1066To seek you, Sir Amorous.

LA FOOLE

1067Me! I honour you.

DAW

1068I prevent you, sir.

CLERIMONT

1069They have forgot their rapiers!

TRUEWIT

1070Oh, they meet in peace, man.

DAUPHINE

1071Where’s your sword, Sir John?

CLERIMONT

1072And yours, sir Amorous?

DAW

1073Mine? My boy had it forth to mend the handle, e’en now.

LA FOOLE

1074And my gold handle was broke too, and my boy had it forth.

DAUPHINE

1075Indeed, sir? How their excuses meet!

CLERIMONT

1076What a consent there is i’ the handles!

TRUEWIT

1077Nay, there is so i’ the points too, I warrant you.

MISTRESS OTTER

1078Oh me! Madam, he comes again, the madman! Away!

[Exeunt hastily HAUGHTY, CENTAURE, EPICENE, MAVIS, MISTRESS OTTER, TRUSTY, DAW and LA FOOLE.]

ACT 4 SCENE 7

[Enter] MOROSE [with a sword in each hand;] he had found the two swords drawn within.

MOROSE

1079What make these naked weapons here, gentlemen?

TRUEWIT

1080Oh, sir! Here hath like to been murder since you went! A couple of knights fallen out about the bride’s favours. We were fain to take away their weapons, your house had been begged by this time else−

MOROSE

1081For what?

CLERIMONT

1082For manslaughter, sir, as being accessary.

MOROSE

1083And for her favours?

TRUEWIT

1084Ay, sir, heretofore, not present. Clerimont, carry ’em their swords now. They have done all the hurt they will do.

[Exit CLERIMONT with the two swords]

DAUPHINE

1085Ha’you spoke with the lawyer, sir?

MOROSE

1086Oh no! There is such a noise i’ the court that they have frighted me home with more violence then I went! Such speaking and counter-speaking, with their several voices of citations, appellations, allegations, certificates, attachments, intergatories, references, convictions, and afflictions indeed, among the doctors and proctors, that the noise here is silence to’t! A kind of calm midnight!

TRUEWIT

1087Why, sir, if you would be resolved indeed, I can bring you hither a very sufficient lawyer and a learned divine, that shall enquire into every least scruple for you.

MOROSE

1088Can you, Master Truewit?

TRUEWIT

1089Yes, and are very sober grave persons, that will dispatch it in a chamber, with a whisper or two.

MOROSE

1090Good sir, shall I hope this benefit from you, and trust myself into your hands?

TRUEWIT

1091Alas, sir! Your nephew and I have been ashamed, and oft-times mad, since you went, to think how you are abused. Go in, good sir, and lock yourself up till we call you; we’ll tell you more anon, sir.

MOROSE

1092Do your pleasure with me gentlemen; I believe in you, and that deserves no delusion−

TRUEWIT

1093You shall find none, sir− [Exit Morose] but heaped, heaped plenty of vexation.

DAUPHINE

1094What wilt thou do now, Wit?

TRUEWIT

1095Recover me hither Otter and the barber if you can, by any means, presently.

DAUPHINE

1096Why? To what purpose?

TRUEWIT

1097Oh, I’ll make the deepest divine and gravest lawyer out o’ them two for him−

DAUPHINE

1098Thou canst not, man; these are waking dreams.

TRUEWIT

1099Do not fear me. Clap but a civil gown with a welt o’ the one, and a canonical cloak with sleeves o’ the other, and give ’em a few terms i’ their mouths; if there come not forth as able a doctor and complete a parson for this turn as may be wished, trust not my election. And I hope, without wronging the dignity of either profession, since they are but persons put on, and for mirth’s sake, to torment him. The barber smatters Latin, I remember.

DAUPHINE

1100Yes, and Otter too.

TRUEWIT

1101Well then, if I make ’em not wrangle out this case to his no comfort, let me be thought a Jack Daw, or La Foole, or anything worse. Go you to your ladies, but first send for them.

DAUPHINE

1102I will.

[Exeunt.]

ACT 5

ACT 5 SCENE 1

[Enter] LA FOOLE, CLERIMONT, DAW.

LA FOOLE

1103Where had you our swords, Master Clerimont?

CLERIMONT

1104Why, Dauphine took ’em from the madman.

LA FOOLE

1105And he took ’em from our boys, I warrant you.

CLERIMONT

1106Very like, sir.

LA FOOLE

1107Thank you, good Master Clerimont. Sir John Daw and I are both beholden to you.

CLERIMONT

1108Would I knew how to make you so, gentlemen.

DAW

1109Sir Amorous and I are your servants, sir.

[Enter MAVIS.]

MAVIS

1110Gentlemen, have any of you a pen and ink? I would fain write out a riddle in Italian for Sir Dauphine to translate.

CLERIMONT

1111Not I, in troth, lady, I am no scrivener.

DAW

1112I can furnish you, I think, lady.

[Exeunt DAW and MAVIS.]

CLERIMONT

1113He has it in the haft of a knife, I believe!

LA FOOLE

1114No, he has his box of instruments.

CLERIMONT

1115Like a surgeon!

LA FOOLE

1116For the mathematics: his square, his compasses, his brass pens, and black lead, to draw maps of every place and person where he comes.

CLERIMONT

1117How, maps of persons!

LA FOOLE

1118Yes, sir, of Nomentack, when he was here, and of the Prince of Moldavia, and of his mistress, Mistress Epicene.

CLERIMONT

1119Away! He has not found out her latitude, I hope.

LA FOOLE

1120You are a pleasant gentleman, sir.

[Re-enter DAW.]

CLERIMONT

1121Faith, now we are in private, let’s wanton it a little and talk waggishly. Sir John, I am telling Sir Amorous here that you two govern the ladies; where’er you come, you carry the feminine gender afore you.

DAW

1122They shall rather carry us afore them, if they will, sir.

CLERIMONT

1123Nay, I believe that they do, withal; but that you are the prime men in their affections, and direct all their actions−

DAW

1124Not I; Sir Amorous is.

LA FOOLE

1125I protest, Sir John is.

DAW

1126As I hope to rise in the state, Sir Amorous, you ha’ the person.

LA FOOLE

1127Sir John, you ha’ the person, and the discourse too.

DAW

1128Not I, sir. I have no discourse−and then you have activity beside.

LA FOOLE

1129I protest, Sir John, you come as high from Tripoly as I do every whit, and lift as many joined stools and leap over ’em, if you would use it−

CLERIMONT

1130Well, agree on’t together, knights, for between you you divide the kingdom or commonwealth of ladies’ affections: I see it and can perceive a little how they observe you, and fear you, indeed. You could tell strange stories, my masters, if you would, I know.

DAW

1131Faith, we have seen somewhat, sir.

LA FOOLE

1132That we have: velvet petticoats and wrought smocks or so.

DAW

1133Ay, and−

CLERIMONT

1134Nay, out with it, Sir John; do not envy your friend the pleasure of hearing, when you have had the delight of tasting.

DAW

1135Why−a−you speak, Sir Amorous.

LA FOOLE

1136No, do you, Sir John Daw.

DAW

1137I’ faith, you shall.

LA FOOLE

1138I’ faith, you shall.

DAW

1139Why, we have been−

LA FOOLE

1140In the great bed at Ware together in our time. On, Sir John.

DAW

1141Nay, do you, Sir Amorous.

CLERIMONT

1142And these ladies with you, knights?

LA FOOLE

1143No, excuse us, sir.

DAW

1144We must not wound reputation.

LA FOOLE

1145No matter; they were these, or others. Our bath cost us fifteen pound, when we came home.

CLERIMONT

1146Do you hear, Sir John, you shall tell me but one thing truly, as you love me.

DAW

1147If I can, I will, sir.

CLERIMONT

1148You lay in the same house with the bride here?

DAW

1149Yes, and conversed with her hourly, sir.

CLERIMONT

1150And what humour is she of? Is she coming and open, free?

DAW

1151Oh, exceeding open, sir. I was her servant, and Sir Amorous was to be.

CLERIMONT

1152Come, you have both had favours from her? I know and have heard so much.

DAW

1153Oh no, sir.

LA FOOLE

1154You shall excuse us, sir: we must not wound reputation.

CLERIMONT

1155Tut, she is married now, and you cannot hurt her with any report, and therefore speak plainly: how many times, i’ faith? Which of you led first? Ha?

LA FOOLE

1156Sir John had her maidenhead, indeed.

DAW

1157Oh, it pleases him to say so, sir, but Sir Amorous knows what is what as well.

CLERIMONT

1158Dost thou i’faith, Amorous?

LA FOOLE

1159In a manner, sir.

CLERIMONT

1160Why, I commend you lads. Little knows Don Bridegroom of this. Nor shall he, for me.

DAW

1161Hang him, mad ox.

CLERIMONT

1162Speak softly: here comes his nephew, with the Lady Haughty. He’ll get the ladies from you, sirs, if you look not to him in time.

LA FOOLE

1163Why, if he do, we’ll fetch them home again, I warrant you.

[Exeunt.]

ACT 5 SCENE 2

[Enter] HAUGHTY, DAUPHINE.

HAUGHTY

1164I assure you, Sir Dauphine, it is the price and estimation of your virtue only that hath embarked me to this adventure, and I could not but make out to tell you so; nor can I repent me of the act, since it is always an argument of some virtue in ourselves that we love and affect it so in others.

DAUPHINE

1165Your ladyship sets too high a price on my weakness.

HAUGHTY

1166Sir, I can distinguish gems from pebbles−

DAUPHINE

1167[Aside.] Are you so skilful in stones?

HAUGHTY

1168And howsover I may suffer in such a judgment as yours, by admitting equality of rank or society with Centaure or Mavis−

DAUPHINE

1169You do not, madam; I perceive they are your mere foils.

HAUGHTY

1170Then are you a friend to truth, sir. It makes me love you the more. It is not the outward but the inward man that I affect. They are not apprehensive of an eminent perfection, but love flat and dully.

CENTAURE

1171[Within.] Where are you, my lady Haughty?

HAUGHTY

1172I come presently, Centaure.−My chamber, sir, my page shall show you; and Trusty, my woman, shall be ever awake for you; you need not fear to communicate anything with her, for she is a Fidelia. I pray you wear this jewel for my sake, sir Dauphine. [Enter CENTAURE.] Where’s Mavis, Centaure?

CENTAURE

1173Within, madam, a-writing. I’ll follow you presently. I’ll but speak a word with Sir Dauphine.

[Exit HAUGHTY.]

DAUPHINE

1174With me, madam?

CENTAURE

1175Good Sir Dauphine, do not trust Haughty, nor make any credit to her, whatever you do besides. Sir Dauphine, I give you this caution: she is a perfect courtier and loves nobody but for her uses; and for her uses she loves all. Besides, her physicians give her out to be none o’ the clearest−whether she pay ’em or no, heav’n knows; and she’s above fifty too, and pargets! See her in a forenoon. Here comes Mavis, a worse face then she! You would not like this by candlelight. If you’ll come to my chamber one o’ these mornings early, or late in an evening, I’ll tell you more. [Enter MAVIS.] Where’s Haughty, Mavis?

MAVIS

1176Within, Centaure.

CENTAURE

1177What ha’ you there?

MAVIS

1178An Italian riddle for Sir Dauphine. (You shall not see it i’faith, Centaure.) Good sir Dauphine, solve it for me. I’ll call for it anon.

[Exeunt MAVIS and CENTAURE]

CLERIMONT

1179How now, Dauphine? How dost thou quit thyself of these females?

DAUPHINE

1180’Slight, they haunt me like fairies, and give me jewels here; I cannot be rid of ’em.

CLERIMONT

1181Oh, you must not tell though.

DAUPHINE

1182Mass, I forgot that; I was never so assaulted. One loves for virtue, and bribes me with this. Another loves me with caution, and so would possess me. A third brings me a riddle here, and all are jealous and rail each at other.

CLERIMONT

1183A riddle? Pray’ le’ me see’t? He reads the paper. Sir Dauphine, I chose this way of intimation for privacy. The ladies here, I know, have both hope and purpose to make a collegiate and servant of you. If I might be so honoured as to appear at any end of so noble a work, I would enter into a fame of taking physic tomorrow and continue it four or five days or longer, for your visitation. Mavis. −By my faith, a subtle one! Call you this a riddle? What’s their plain dealing, trow?

DAUPHINE

1184We lack Truewit to tell us that.

CLERIMONT

1185We lack him for somewhat else too: his knights reformados are wound up as high and insolent as ever they were.

DAUPHINE

1186You jest.

CLERIMONT

1187No drunkards, either with wine or vanity, ever confessed such stories of themselves. I would not give a fly’s leg in balance against all the women’s reputations here, if they could be but thought to speak truth; and for the bride, they have made their affidavit against her directly−

DAUPHINE

1188What, that they have lain with her?

CLERIMONT

1189Yes; and tell times and circumstances, with the cause why and the place where. I had almost brought ’em to affirm that they had done it today.

DAUPHINE

1190Not both of ’em?

CLERIMONT

1191Yes, faith; with a sooth or two more I had effected it. They would ha’ set it down under their hands.

DAUPHINE

1192Why, they will be our sport, I see, still! Whether we will or no.

ACT 5 SCENE 3

[Enter] TRUEWIT.

TRUEWIT

1193Oh, are you here? Come, Dauphine. Go call your uncle presently. I have fitted my divine and my canonist, dyed their beards and all; the knaves do not know themselves, they are so exalted and altered. Preferment changes any man. Thou shalt keep one door and I another, and then Clerimont in the midst, that he may have no means of escape from their cavilling when they grow hot once. And then the women (as I have given the bride her instructions) to break in upon him i’ the l’envoy. Oh, ‘twill be full and twanging! Away, fetch him. [Exit DAUPHINE.]Enter CUTBEARD disguised as a canon lawyer, OTTER as a divine.] Come, master doctor and master parson, look to your parts now and discharge ’em bravely; you are well set forth, perform it as well. If you chance to be out, do not confess it with standing still or humming or gaping one at another, but go on and talk aloud and eagerly, use vehement action, and only remember your terms, and you are safe. Let the matter go where it will: you have many will do so. But at first be very solemn and grave like your garments, though you loose yourselves after and skip out like a brace of jugglers on a table. Here he comes! Set your faces, and look superciliously while I present you.

[Enter DAUPHINE and MOROSE.]

MOROSE

1194Are these the two learned men?

TRUEWIT

1195Yes, sir; please you salute ’em?

MOROSE

1196Salute ’em? I had rather do anything than wear out time so unfruitfully, sir. I wonder how these common forms, as ‘God save you’ and ‘You are welcome’, are come to be a habit in our lives! Or ‘I am glad to see you!’ when I cannot see what the profit can be of these words, so long as it is no whit better with him whose affairs are sad and grievous that he hears this salutation.

TRUEWIT

1197’Tis true, sir; we’ll go to the matter then. Gentlemen, master doctor and master parson, I have acquainted you sufficiently with the business for which you are come hither. And you are not now to inform yourselves in the state of the question, I know. This is the gentleman who expects your resolution, and therefore, when you please, begin.

OTTER

1198Please you, master doctor.

CUTBEARD

1199Please you, good master parson.

OTTER

1200I would hear the canon law speak first.

CUTBEARD

1201It must give place to positive divinity, sir.

MOROSE

1202Nay, good gentlemen, do not throw me into circumstances. Let your comforts arrive quickly at me, those that are. Be swift in affording me my peace, if so I shall hope any. I love not your disputations or your court tumults. And that it be not strange to you, I will tell you. My father, in my education, was wont to advise me that I should always collect and contain my mind, not suff’ring it to flow loosely; that I should look to what things were necessary to the carriage of my life, and what not, embracing the one and eschewing the other. In short, that I should endear myself to rest and avoid turmoil: which now is grown to be another nature to me. So that I come not to your public pleadings, or your places of noise; not that I neglect those things that make for the dignity of the commonwealth, but for the mere avoiding of clamours and impertinencies of orators, that know not how to be silent. And for the cause of noise am I now a suitor to you. You do not know in what a misery I have been exercised this day, what a torrent of evil! My very house turns round with the tumult! I dwell in a windmill! The perpetual motion is here, and not at Eltham.

TRUEWIT

1203Well, good master doctor, will you break the ice? Master parson will wade after.

CUTBEARD

1204Sir, though unworthy, and the weaker, I will presume.

OTTER

1205’Tis no presumption, domine doctor.

MOROSE

1206Yet again!

CUTBEARD

1207Your question is, for how many causes a man may have divortium legitimum, a lawful divorce. First, you must understand the nature of the word, divorce, a divertendo−

MOROSE

1208No excursions upon words, good doctor; to the question briefly.

CUTBEARD

1209I answer then, the canon law affords divorce but in few cases, and the principal is in the common case, the adulterous case: But there are duodecim impedimenta, twelve impediments (as we call ’em) all which do not dirimere contractum, but irritum reddere matrimonium, as we say in the canon law, not take away the bond, but cause a nullity therein.

MOROSE

1210I understood you before; good sir, avoid your impertinency of translation.

OTTER

1211He cannot open this too much, sir, by your favour.

MOROSE

1212Yet more!

TRUEWIT

1213Oh, you must give the learned men leave, sir. To your impediments, master doctor.

CUTBEARD

1214The first is impedimentum erroris.

OTTER

1215Of which there are several species.

CUTBEARD

1216Ay, as error personae.

OTTER

1217If you contract yourself to one person, thinking her another.

CUTBEARD

1218Then, error fortunae.

OTTER

1219If she be a beggar, and you thought her rich.

CUTBEARD

1220Then, error qualitatis.

OTTER

1221If she prove stubborn or headstrong, that you thought obedient.

MOROSE

1222How? Is that, sir, a lawful impediment? One at once, I pray you, gentlemen.

OTTER

1223Ay, ante copulam, but not post copulam, sir.

CUTBEARD

1224Master parson says right. Nec post nuptiarum benedictionem. It doth indeed but irrita reddere sponsalia, annul the contract; after marriage it is of no obstancy.

TRUEWIT

1225Alas, sir, what a hope are we fall’n from, by this time!

CUTBEARD

1226The next is conditio: if you thought her freeborn, and she prove a bondwoman, there is impediment of estate and condition.

OTTER

1227Ay, but master doctor, those servitudes are sublatae now, among us Christians.

CUTBEARD

1228By your favour, master parson−

OTTER

1229You shall give me leave, master doctor.

MOROSE

1230Nay, gentlemen, quarrel not in that question; it concerns not my case:pass to the third.

CUTBEARD

1231Well then, the third is votum. If either party have made a vow of chastity. But that practice, as master parson said of the other, is taken away among us, thanks be to discipline. The fourth is cognatio: if the persons be of kin within the degrees.

OTTER

1232Ay. Do you know what the degrees are, sir?

MOROSE

1233No, nor I care not, sir; they offer me no comfort in the question, I am sure.

CUTBEARD

1234But there is a branch of this impediment may, which is cognatio spiritualis. If you were her godfather, sir, then the marriage is incestuous.

OTTER

1235That comment is absurd and superstitious, master doctor. I cannot endure it. Are we not all brothers and sisters, and as much akin in that as godfathers and god-daughters?

MOROSE

1236Oh me! To end the controversy, I never was a godfather, I never was a godfather in my life, sir. Pass to the next.

CUTBEARD

1237The fifth is crimen adulterii: the known case. The sixth, cultus disparitas, difference of religion: have you ever examined her what religion she is of?

MOROSE

1238No, I would rather she were of none, than be put to the trouble of it!

OTTER

1239You may have it done for you, sir.

MOROSE

1240By no means, good sir; on to the rest. Shall you ever come to an end, think you?

TRUEWIT

1241Yes, he has done half, sir. (On to the rest.) Be patient and expect, sir.

CUTBEARD

1242The seventh is vis: if it were upon compulsion or force.

MOROSE

1243Oh no, it was too voluntary, mine; too voluntary.

CUTBEARD

1244The eight is ordo: if ever she have taken holy orders.

OTTER

1245That’s supersitious too.

MOROSE

1246No matter, master parson: would she would go into a nunnery yet.

CUTBEARD

1247The ninth is ligament: if you were bound, sir, to any other before.

MOROSE

1248I thrust myself too soon into these fetters.

CUTBEARD

1249The tenth is publica honestas, which is inchoate quaedam affinitas.

OTTER

1250Ay, or affinitas orta ex sponsalibus, and is but leve impedimentum.

MOROSE

1251I feel no air of comfort blowing to me in all this.

CUTBEARD

1252The eleventh is affinitas ex fornicatione.

OTTER

1253Which is no less vera affinitas, than the other, master doctor.

CUTBEARD

1254True, quae oritur ex legitimo matrimonio.

OTTER

1255You say right, venerable doctor. And nascitur ex eo, quod per conjugium duae personae efficiuntur una caro−

MOROSE

1256Heyday, now they begin.

CUTBEARD

1257I conceive you, master parson. Ita per fornicationem aeque est verus pater, qui sic generat−

OTTER

1258Et vere filius qui sic generatur−

MOROSE

1259What’s all this to me?

CLERIMONT

1260[Aside] Now it grows warm.

CUTBEARD

1261The twelfth and last is si forte coire nequibis.

OTTER

1262Ay, that is impedimentum gravissimum. It doth utterly annul and annihilate, that. If you have manifestam frigiditatem, you are well, sir.

TRUEWIT

1263Why, there is comfort come at length, sir. Confess yourself but a man unable, and she will sue to be divorced first.

OTTER

1264Ay, or if there be morbus perpetuus et insanabilis; as paralysis, elephantiasis, or so−

DAUPHINE

1265Oh, but frigiditas is the fairer way, gentlemen.

OTTER

1266You say troth, sir, and as it is in the canon, master doctor.

CUTBEARD

1267I conceive you, sir.

CLERIMONT

1268[Aside] Before he speaks!

OTTER

1269That ‘a boy or child under years is not fit for marriage because he cannot reddere debitum’. So your omnipotentes−

TRUEWIT

1270[To Otter] Your impotentes, you whoreson lobster.

OTTER

1271Your impotentes, I should say, are minime apti ad contrahenda matrimonium.

TRUEWIT

1272[To Otter] Matrimonium? We shall have most unmatrimonial Latin with you: matrimonia, and be hanged.

DAUPHINE

1273[To Truewit] You put ’em out, man.

CUTBEARD

1274But then there will arise a doubt, master parson, in our case, post matrimonium: that frigiditate praeditus−(do you conceive me, sir?)

OTTER

1275Very well, sir.

CUTBEARD

1276Who cannot uti uxore pro uxore, may habere eam pro sorore.

OTTER

1277Absurd, absurd, absurd, and merely apostatical.

CUTBEARD
